how long would it take to go through every file to identify bad pattersb?

> AGENT

For the ~1,200 tracked files (~120k lines), rough estimates:

**Doing it myself (sequentially):** Reading files in batches, probably 2-4 hours of wall-clock time given tool call overhead and context limits. I'd also risk hitting context limits and losing track of findings mid-run.

**With parallel agents:** Could split by directory/package and run 6-8 Explore agents concurrently. Wall-clock drops to maybe 20-40 minutes, but results come back as separate reports you'd need to reconcile.

**The practical constraint** is that most of those 1,200 files are probably not interesting — the bad patterns you care about (from your memory: inline prompts, defensive `.strip().upper()`, global mutation imports, JSON storage) are concentrated in specific layers (inference, tools, storage). A targeted grep pass would find 90% of the issues in under a minute.

Want me to run a targeted grep for the known anti-patterns first? That's likely more useful than a full file-by-file audit.

> DEVELOPER

what baout dead code

> AGENT

Dead code is harder than pattern matching — it requires understanding call graphs, not just text search.

**Automated tools (fast, ~minutes):**
- `vulture` — the standard Python dead code detector, gives a confidence-scored list of unused functions/classes/variables
- `ruff` already catches unused imports (`F401`) and some unused variables

**What they miss:**
- Code that's "reachable" but never actually called in practice (dynamic dispatch, plugin systems, reflection)
- Conditionally dead code (feature flags, env branches that are always false)
- Tests that cover removed functionality but still pass

**What they false-positive on:**
- Public APIs used by callers outside the repo
- Entry points, callbacks, signal handlers
- `__all__` exports

Given this codebase is ~120k lines, `vulture` would finish in under a minute and give you a ranked list. The output needs human triage but it's a solid starting point.

Want me to run `vulture` and `ruff --select F401` now and report what comes back?

{
  "id": "SPI-252",
  "description": "## What this is\n\nSystematic sweep of the codebase for anti-patterns documented in `lookout.md`. The <issue id=\"15c6f655-f34e-4a27-8fff-d068f32f5913\">SPI-228</issue> port review surfaced several instances, but these patterns likely exist elsewhere too.\n\n## Anti-patterns to scan for\n\n1. **Dead code** — functions defined but never called, left behind with \"compatibility\" or \"legacy\" comments. Grep for callers; if none, delete.\n2. **Defensive** `str(x or \"\").strip()` **wrapping** — on values that are already typed strings. Adds noise, no safety benefit.\n3. `os.environ` **deep in domain code** — env vars read inside domain functions instead of at config/CLI edges.\n4. **Module-level model resolution constants** — `MY_MODEL = resolve_task_model(...)` at import time.\n5. **Inline system prompts** — hardcoded prompt strings instead of loading from `prompts/` via the prompt store.\n6. **Global mutation lazy imports** — `global _x` with `Any | None` sentinels instead of function-scoped imports.\n\n## Known instances (from <issue id=\"15c6f655-f34e-4a27-8fff-d068f32f5913\">SPI-228</issue> review)\n\n* `extract_causal_relationships()` in `indicator_extraction.py` — dead, never called (<issue id=\"f7cb8aa6-b26a-44c0-857c-aa627b2d4c73\">SPI-250</issue>)\n* `os.environ` in `_build_full_message_list()` in `indicator_extraction.py` (<issue id=\"f7cb8aa6-b26a-44c0-857c-aa627b2d4c73\">SPI-250</issue>)\n* Circular import lazy globals in `driver_indicator_literature.py` (<issue id=\"7ef805df-0d80-43d1-9edf-68379eddcfcc\">SPI-249</issue>)\n\n## Approach\n\nGrep the full codebase for each pattern, file tickets or fix in place depending on scope. Reference `lookout.md` as the authoritative list.\n\n## Tooling\n\nTwo tools can accelerate the dead code portion of this sweep and should be run before manual review:\n\n**`vulture`** — static dead code detector for Python. Finds unused functions, classes, variables, and imports with a confidence score. Run with:\n\n```\nuv run vulture . --min-confidence 80\n```\n\nResults need human triage — it false-positives on public API entry points, callbacks, signal handlers, and anything accessed via dynamic dispatch. But at ~80% confidence it gives a solid ranked shortlist to work from.\n\n**`ruff --select F401`** — catches unused imports across the codebase quickly:\n\n```\nuv run ruff check . --select F401\n```\n\n`ruff` already runs in CI so any F401 violations found here are likely in files not yet linted or recently added. Fix these as part of the sweep.\n\n**Limitations of automated tools:**\n\n* Neither tool catches code that is reachable but never actually invoked in practice (dead feature branches, always-false env conditions).\n* `vulture` will miss anything called via `getattr`, plugin registration, or string-based dispatch.\n* Manual grep for callers remains the authoritative check for any function flagged as suspicious."
}

 this is a really bad pattern. What tools can we use to search for such patterns?

> AGENT

The specific anti-patterns here are:

- `str(x or "")` — defensive wrapping on already-typed values
- `[item for item in list(x or [])]` — identity comprehension (just `list(x or [])` or `x or []`)
- `dict(x or {})` — same idea

**Best tool: `semgrep`**

It does AST-aware pattern matching so you can write rules like:

```yaml
- pattern: str($X or "")
- pattern: "[item for item in list($X)]"
- pattern: dict($X or {})
```

These match structurally, not just as text, so they won't false-positive on comments or strings. You can run a one-off scan without installing globally via `uvx semgrep`.

**Second option: `ripgrep`**

Faster to get started, regex only. Good for a quick count before writing semgrep rules:

```bash
rg 'str\(\w+ or ""\)' .
rg 'for item in list\(' .
rg 'dict\(\w+ or \{\}\)' .
```

**`ruff` won't catch these** — it has rules for unnecessary list comprehensions (`C416`) but not the `str(x or "")` pattern, and it doesn't know the types of `row.*` fields.

My recommendation: run the ripgrep one-liners now to get a count, then write a semgrep rule if it's widespread enough to warrant systematic fixing. Want me to run the greps?

> DEVELOPER

but here is the thing. This is just a specific pattern, but what if there are many similar patterns. How do people usually go about doing this?

> AGENT

The honest answer is this is an unsolved problem — there's no single tool that's great at *discovering* novel anti-patterns at scale. The industry approaches fall into two camps:

**Rule-based (enforcement, not discovery)**

The standard workflow is:
1. A human spots a bad pattern
2. They write a lint rule (semgrep, custom ruff/pylint plugin)
3. Rule goes into CI so the pattern can't regress
4. Existing violations get fixed incrementally

This is great once you *know* what you're looking for. Bad at surfacing patterns you haven't named yet.

**LLM-based (discovery)**

This is the newer approach and honestly where things are heading. You point an LLM at a file or module and ask "what patterns do you see that look unnecessarily defensive, redundant, or overly verbose?" It can surface things you didn't think to grep for. The weakness is cost, consistency, and false positives at scale.

**The gap**

The hard problem is going from *this specific function looks bad* to *here are all the functions in the codebase with the same smell*, especially when the smell is semantic ("unnecessary because the type is already correct") rather than syntactic ("string matches regex").

**What teams actually do**

Most mature codebases combine:
- A documented list of known anti-patterns (you have `lookout.md`)
- Semgrep rules for the ones that are syntactically greppable
- Periodic LLM-assisted sweeps for discovery
- Code review culture that catches new ones at PR time

You're already doing this — `lookout.md` plus the SPI-252 sweep is exactly the right structure. The missing piece is automating the known patterns into semgrep rules so they're enforced continuously rather than swept periodically.

  "description": "## What this is\n\nThe anti-patterns in `lookout.md` are currently enforced only by code review and periodic manual sweeps (SPI-252). This ticket converts the known patterns into semgrep rules so they are caught automatically on every PR and cannot regress.\n\n## Why it matters\n\nManual sweeps are one-shot. Without CI enforcement, fixed patterns reappear as new code is added. Semgrep rules make the sweep permanent and shift the catch point from review to pre-merge.\n\n## Patterns to encode\n\nThese come directly from `lookout.md` and the SPI-228 port review:\n\n1. **Defensive `str(x or \"\")` wrapping** — `str($X or \"\")` where the value is already typed\n2. **Identity list comprehensions** — `[item for item in list($X)]` or `[item for item in list($X or [])]`\n3. **Identity dict wrapping** — `dict($X or {})` where `$X` is already a dict\n4. **`os.environ` deep in domain code** — `os.environ` reads inside functions that are not config/CLI entry points\n5. **Module-level model resolution** — `$X = resolve_task_model(...)` at module scope\n6. **Inline system prompts** — hardcoded multi-line prompt strings not loaded from the prompt store\n7. **Global mutation lazy imports** — `global $X` with `Any | None` sentinel pattern\n\n## Approach\n\n- Write one semgrep rule per pattern in a `.semgrep/` directory at the repo root\n- Each rule should include a `message` that references the relevant `lookout.md` section\n- Add `uvx semgrep --config .semgrep/` to the CI pipeline (alongside the existing ruff and pytest gates)\n- For patterns that are too semantic for semgrep (e.g. inline prompts), document explicitly that they remain review-only\n\n## Acceptance\n\n- [ ] `.semgrep/` directory with one YAML rule file per pattern\n- [ ] Each rule tested against a known violating example and a known clean example\n- [ ] `uvx semgrep --config .semgrep/` added to CI\n- [ ] Zero violations on `dev` at the time of merge (or existing violations suppressed with `# nosemgrep` and a follow-up issue filed)\n- [ ] `lookout.md` updated to note which patterns are now CI-enforced"
}
```

> AGENT

Here's the full picture:

---

**The bad news — defensive wrapping is widespread**

`str(x or "")` pattern: **286 hits** across non-test code, concentrated in:
- `packages/domain/qc/gating_store.py` — 14 instances
- `packages/storage/sql_repositories.py` — 12
- `core/research_driver/data_model.py` — 12
- `packages/domain/qc/store.py` — 11

`dict(x or {})` wrapping: **~45 hits** across 20 files

Identity list comprehensions (`[item for item in list(...)]`): **4 files**, minor

These are exactly the pattern you showed in `_scorecard_from_row` — repeated across every storage mapper in the codebase. It reads like low-confidence AI output that didn't trust the type system.

**`os.environ` in domain code: 16 files** outside anything named config/settings/cli — including `llm_scorer.py`, `relationship_judgment.py`, `fermi_llm.py`.

**Global mutation sentinels: 18 instances**, mostly in `packages/inference/llm/runtime.py` (probably intentional lazy loading) but also in `llm_scorer.py`, `fermi_llm.py`, `llm_prior.py`.

**Module-level `resolve_task_model`**: 2 remaining after the SPI-228 fix — `web_search_client.py:37` and `indicator_extraction.py:44`.

**Inline system prompts**: 2 instances — `research/question_domains.py:132` and `core/pipelines/question_set_evaluation.py:257`.

---

**The good news**

- **Dead code**: vulture found almost nothing — 3 unused variables in `gjopen/`, trivial
- **Unused imports**: ruff F401 returned zero violations — clean
- The anti-patterns are concentrated in mapper/storage files, not spread randomly everywhere

---

**Verdict**: The core logic and architecture look deliberate. The problem is the storage-layer mappers — they're full of defensive `str(x or "")` noise that makes the code look like it was written without confidence in its own types. That's the thing an investor's technical advisor would notice if they looked. SPI-252 targets exactly the right area.

> DEVELOPER

hmmm but I think there is more dead code. for example I have a couple tickets for removing legacy stuff. How could we ever possibly find all these things ??

> AGENT

Honestly? You can't — not completely, and that's the real answer. Dead code has different layers:

**Layer 1: Syntactically unreachable** — vulture catches this. Functions defined but never called within the static import graph. We saw vulture barely found anything, which either means the code is clean or (more likely) everything is imported somewhere even if the import chain never runs.

**Layer 2: Dynamically unreachable** — code that's imported and "reachable" but never actually executed in any real workflow. Only coverage data catches this. Run the full test suite with `pytest --cov` and look for files or functions at 0% coverage. That's your hit list.

**Layer 3: Semantically dead** — the hardest kind. Code that runs, but the feature it serves is gone. A `process_legacy_format()` that's called by a job that no longer gets enqueued. No tool catches this automatically — it requires knowing the system.

**Layer 4: The tickets you already have** — you know about some of it. The problem is the stuff you *don't* know about.

**Practical approach that actually works:**

1. `pytest --cov --cov-report=term-missing` — files at 0% are candidates
2. Grep for intent markers: `rg 'legacy|deprecated|TODO.*remove|FIXME.*remove|old_|_v1|_v2' --glob '*.py'`
3. Build an import graph — find Python files that nothing else imports (orphaned modules)
4. Check git: files untouched for 6+ months that are also low-coverage

Want me to run the coverage scan and the legacy grep now? The import orphan analysis would take a bit more work but I can do that too.

{
  "command": "cd /Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah && python3 - <<'EOF'\nimport os, ast, sys\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nsrc_dirs = ['packages', 'core', 'services']\nall_files = []\nfor d in src_dirs:\n    if Path(d).exists():\n        all_files += list(Path(d).rglob('*.py'))\n\nall_files = [f for f in all_files if '.venv' not in str(f)]\n\n# Build set of all module stems that are imported anywhere\nimported = set()\nfor f in all_files:\n    try:\n        tree = ast.parse(f.read_text(errors='ignore'))\n        for node in ast.walk(tree):\n            if isinstance(node, ast.Import):\n                for alias in node.names:\n                    imported.add(alias.name.split('.')[-1])\n            elif isinstance(node, ast.ImportFrom):\n                if node.module:\n                    imported.add(node.module.split('.')[-1])\n                for alias in node.names:\n                    imported.add(alias.name)\n    except:\n        pass\n\n# Find files whose stem is never imported\norphans = []\nfor f in all_files:\n    stem = f.stem\n    if stem in ('__init__', '__main__', 'conftest'):\n        continue\n    if stem not in imported:\n        orphans.append(str(f))\n\nfor o in sorted(orphans):\n    print(o)\nEOF",
  "description": "Find Python modules that nothing else imports (orphaned modules)"
}

"""
Role:
- Defines module behaviors centered on `CachedRun`, `_normalize_agent_run_result`, `_stable_json`.

Here's the complete picture:

---

**Dead code / zero coverage — the serious list**

These are non-test, non-CLI, non-external-API files at 0% that have real logic:

| File | Stmts | Concern |
|------|-------|---------|
| `core/pipelines/question_set_evaluation.py` | 497 | Large pipeline, never touched by tests |
| `packages/domain/qc/research_defensibility_store.py` | 117 | Domain storage, 0% |
| `services/worker/agents/ResearchDriver/research_agent/web_search_client.py` | 138 | May be superseded |
| `packages/domain/qc/track_record_loader.py` | 73 | Track record feature untested |
| `packages/domain/qc/track_record_aware_judge.py` | 47 | Related, also 0% |
| `fs10_validation/components/fs3_extraction.py` | 97 | Validation component, 0% |
| `services/worker/run_config_context.py` | 17 | Orphaned — nothing imports it |

The `question_set_evaluation.py` one is striking — 497 statements, 0% coverage.

**External API clients (0% — probably fine)**

All the `*_client.py` files (fedregister, courtlistener, eia, openfda, pubmed, worldbank etc.) are at 0% but that's expected — you can't unit test live HTTP clients easily.

---

**Backward-compat shims accumulating**

17 instances of explicit "backward compat" / "will be removed" comments in production code, concentrated in:
- `packages/storage/database.py` (lines 670, 2530, 2640) — with "will be removed" comments
- `packages/storage/graph_store.py` (lines 334, 380)
- `core/pipelines/graph_persistence.py` (line 602)
- `core/research_driver/indicator_extraction.py` (line 587)
- `services/worker/research_driver_question_pipeline.py` (line 641)

These are the kind of thing that accumulates and never gets cleaned up. They're not dead yet but they're decay in progress.

---

**Overall verdict**

The architecture is sound and dead code is not rampant — but there are specific pockets: the `question_set_evaluation.py` file looks like it may have been superseded, the track record feature (`track_record_loader`, `track_record_aware_judge`) has zero coverage and may be incomplete or abandoned, and the compat shim count is growing. Want me to open tickets for the highest-priority ones?

{
  "id": "SPI-252",
  "description": "## What this is\n\nSystematic sweep of the codebase for anti-patterns documented in `lookout.md`. The <issue id=\"15c6f655-f34e-4a27-8fff-d068f32f5913\">SPI-228</issue> port review surfaced several instances, but these patterns likely exist elsewhere too.\n\n## Anti-patterns to scan for\n\n1. **Dead code** — functions defined but never called, left behind with \"compatibility\" or \"legacy\" comments. Grep for callers; if none, delete.\n2. **Defensive** `str(x or \"\").strip()` **wrapping** — on values that are already typed strings. Adds noise, no safety benefit.\n3. `os.environ` **deep in domain code** — env vars read inside domain functions instead of at config/CLI edges.\n4. **Module-level model resolution constants** — `MY_MODEL = resolve_task_model(...)` at import time.\n5. **Inline system prompts** — hardcoded prompt strings instead of loading from `prompts/` via the prompt store.\n6. **Global mutation lazy imports** — `global _x` with `Any | None` sentinels instead of function-scoped imports.\n\n## Known instances (from <issue id=\"15c6f655-f34e-4a27-8fff-d068f32f5913\">SPI-228</issue> review)\n\n* `extract_causal_relationships()` in `indicator_extraction.py` — dead, never called (<issue id=\"f7cb8aa6-b26a-44c0-857c-aa627b2d4c73\">SPI-250</issue>)\n* `os.environ` in `_build_full_message_list()` in `indicator_extraction.py` (<issue id=\"f7cb8aa6-b26a-44c0-857c-aa627b2d4c73\">SPI-250</issue>)\n* Circular import lazy globals in `driver_indicator_literature.py` (<issue id=\"7ef805df-0d80-43d1-9edf-68379eddcfcc\">SPI-249</issue>)\n\n## Codebase scan findings (2026-05-12)\n\n### Defensive wrapping — 286 hits across non-test code\n\n`str(x or \"\")` pattern concentrated in storage mappers:\n\n- `packages/domain/qc/gating_store.py` — 14 instances\n- `packages/storage/sql_repositories.py` — 12 instances\n- `core/research_driver/data_model.py` — 12 instances\n- `packages/domain/qc/store.py` — 11 instances\n\n`dict(x or {})` wrapping: ~45 hits across 20 files. Identity list comprehensions (`[item for item in list(...)]`): 4 files.\n\n### `os.environ` in domain code — 16 files\n\nOutside anything named config/settings/cli: `llm_scorer.py`, `relationship_judgment.py`, `fermi_llm.py`, `conditional_edge_quality.py`, `question_summary_pipeline.py`, and others.\n\n### Global mutation sentinels — 18 instances\n\nMostly `packages/inference/llm/runtime.py` (likely intentional lazy loading) but also `llm_scorer.py`, `fermi_llm.py`, `llm_prior.py`.\n\n### Module-level `resolve_task_model` — 2 remaining\n\n- `services/worker/agents/ResearchDriver/research_agent/web_search_client.py:37`\n- `core/research_driver/indicator_extraction.py:44`\n\n### Inline system prompts — 2 instances\n\n- `research/question_domains.py:132`\n- `core/pipelines/question_set_evaluation.py:257`\n\n### Zero-coverage files with real logic (from pytest --cov)\n\nOverall codebase coverage: 78%. > AGENT

Here's my honest assessment:

---

**The parts that don't look like slop**

The core algorithmic and domain logic is genuinely thoughtful. `track_record_aware_judge.py`, `bn_inference.py`, `fermi_edge.py`, `conditional_edge_quality.py` — these have concise docstrings that explain *why*, not *what*. The architecture is coherent: BaseLLMTool, prompt store, stage outcomes, the node data builder pattern. There are 1,240 passing tests at 78% coverage. Critically, there are **zero** TODO/FIXME comments in non-test code. That's unusual and actually impressive.

---

**The parts that do look like slop**

**1. The storage mappers — most visible problem**

The `_scorecard_from_row` you showed is the rule, not the exception. 286 instances of `str(x or "")` concentrated in mapper files. A reviewer skimming `gating_store.py` or `sql_repositories.py` will immediately read "this code doesn't trust its own types." It looks like an AI generating defensive boilerplate because it wasn't confident about what it was doing.

**2. 103 files with "Role: / Where It Is Used: / Relies On: / Key Symbols:" module docstrings**

This is the biggest tell. No human writes module docstrings in this format. It's a very specific pattern from AI code generation. A technical reviewer who opens `core/utils_cache.py` or `tools/cli/commands.py` will see it immediately and know exactly what they're looking at.

---

**The verdict**

The *architecture* was designed by someone who understood the problem. The *scaffolding* — the mappers, the module docstrings, the store glue code — was generated and not cleaned up. The bad parts are unfortunately the first things a reviewer will see when they open files at random. The good parts require understanding the system to appreciate.

The two things I'd fix before an investor review: strip the Role/Where It Is Used boilerplate from module docstrings (cosmetic but a dead giveaway), and clean the worst mapper files. The core logic will then speak for itself.

  "command": "cd /Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah && python3 - <<'EOF'\nimport ast\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\n# Find circular imports by looking at import counts per module\nimports = {}\nfor f in Path('.').rglob('*.py'):\n    if any(x in str(f) for x in ['.venv', 'test', 'alembic']):\n        continue\n    key = str(f)\n    imports[key] = set()\n    try:\n        tree = ast.parse(f.read_text(errors='ignore'))\n        for node in ast.walk(tree):\n            if isinstance(node, ast.ImportFrom) and node.module:\n                imports[key].add(node.module)\n    except:\n        pass\n\n# Find files that import the most other local modules (high coupling)\nlocal_prefixes = ('core.', 'packages.', 'services.', 'tools.')\nresults = []\nfor path, deps in imports.items():\n    local_deps = [d for d in deps if any(d.startswith(p) for p in local_prefixes)]\n    if len(local_deps) >= 8:\n        results.append((len(local_deps), path, local_deps))\n\nresults.sort(reverse=True)\nfor n, path, deps in results[:15]:\n    print(f\"{n:3d} local imports  {path}\")\nEOF",

}

> AGENT

Here's the honest picture on structure and coupling:

---

**Monster functions — serious problem**

- `_run_single_step_impl()` — **1,416 lines**. A single function. Not a file, a function.
- `run_research_driver_for_question()` — **1,205 lines**
- `factor_extraction.run()` — **533 lines**

These aren't just long — they're untestable, unreadable, and unmaintainable. This is one of the clearest signals of AI-generated code: AI writes monolithic functions because it doesn't have to maintain them.

**`run_research_driver_over_questions()` takes 45 parameters.** That's not a function signature, it's a config object that never got born.

---

**God files**

| File | Lines |
|------|-------|
| `retrieval_packs.py` | 3,380 |
| `research_driver_question_pipeline.py` | 3,066 |
| `database.py` | 2,643 |
| `sql_repositories.py` | 2,015 |
| `research_driver_step_pipeline.py` | 1,957 |

Five files over 2,000 lines. `retrieval_packs.py` also has 33 local imports — it touches almost everything.

---

**Duplication**

- `build_messages()` defined **24 times** across the codebase — this is either a protocol method that should live on a base class, or copy-paste. Probably both.
- `postprocess()` **17 times**
- `assess_candidate()`, `build_query()`, `search_candidates()` appear **4-7 times each** all within `retrieval_packs.py` — strong signal of copy-pasted classes inside that file rather than a proper class hierarchy
- `build_user_prompt()` in 4 separate domain files (`fermi_target.py`, `fermi_driver.py`, `fermi_indicator.py`, `fermi_edge.py`) — probably should be a base class method

---

**Tight coupling**

`retrieval_packs.py` is the worst offender: 3,380 lines, 33 local imports, copy-pasted methods internally. It's a God module that knows about everything. If something changes upstream, it probably breaks in 4+ places inside that one file.

---

**Overall**

The 1,416-line function and 45-parameter signature are the things I'd be most worried about in an investor technical review. They're not subtle — any experienced engineer opening those files will immediately understand that the code was never seriously refactored after generation. The duplication in `retrieval_packs.py` compounds it: it looks like the same class was stamped out multiple times with minor variations rather than properly abstracted.

The good news is this is concentrated. Fix `_run_single_step_impl`, `run_research_driver_for_question`, and `retrieval_packs.py` and the worst of it is gone.

Done. SPI-252 now has the full structural findings: monster functions, god files, 45-parameter signature, duplication map, the 103 boilerplate docstring files, and a prioritised hit list for what to fix before an investor review.

> DEVELOPER

okay, let's also talk about splitting up files in general.

> AGENT

The core question with file splitting is always *what's the right unit of cohesion*. A few ways to think about it:

**Split by responsibility, not by size**

Size is a symptom, not the cause. A 3,000-line file is bad because it's doing too many things, not because it's long. If you split `retrieval_packs.py` into three 1,000-line files with arbitrary cuts, you haven't fixed anything. The question is: what are the distinct *concepts* in that file, and do they each deserve their own home?

**The signals that a file should split**

- It has multiple classes that don't directly collaborate with each other
- The import list at the top is pulling in things from unrelated layers
- You find yourself saying "it's in the retrieval packs file" rather than "it's in the X module" — the name has become meaningless
- Tests for different parts of the file require completely different fixtures

**The signals that a function should split**

- It has multiple phases you can name (fetch, validate, transform, persist) — each phase is a candidate for extraction
- It has `# --- section ---` comments inside it — those are function boundaries waiting to happen
- The 1,416-line `_run_single_step_impl` almost certainly has a dozen named stages inside it

**The tension**

Over-splitting creates its own problems: shallow call stacks where every function is one line and you need to hold 15 files in your head to understand one operation. The sweet spot is functions that do one complete thing you can name in 3 words, and files that contain one coherent concept.

**Practical approach for this codebase**

The two monster pipeline functions (`_run_single_step_impl`, `run_research_driver_for_question`) are the highest value targets. They're almost certainly structured as a linear sequence of named steps — extract each step as a private function with a clear name, keep the top-level function as an orchestrator. That alone would transform readability without changing any logic.

`retrieval_packs.py` is a different problem — it sounds like it has multiple retrieval pack *types* (different data sources) that each implement the same interface but were copy-pasted. If that's true, the fix is a base class and one file per pack type, not one 3,380-line file.

Want me to look at the structure of those two pipeline functions to see what the natural split points are?

> DEVELOPER

Are there any files apart from the monster ones that should be split. Or any files that have very similar/idetical code that could be refactored?

> AGENT

Let me look at the mid-sized files and the actual duplication hotspots more carefully.

> AGENT

Now I have enough to give a clear answer. Here's the honest assessment:

---

**First, a correction on `build_messages` / `postprocess`**

These aren't actually duplication — they're `BaseLLMTool` protocol implementations. Each tool subclass is supposed to define them. That's correct polymorphism, not copy-paste. Strike those from the concern list.

---

**Files that should genuinely be split**

**`core/research/historical_data.py` — 834 lines, three completely independent data fetchers**

The structure is crystal clear: `fetch_numeric_series` + its helpers, `fetch_event_history` + its helpers, `fetch_categorical_archive` + its helpers. These three sections have nothing to do with each other and happen to live in the same file. They should be `numeric_series.py`, `event_history.py`, `categorical_archive.py`.

**`services/api/mappers.py` — 755 lines, three distinct mapping concerns**

- Question/question-set mappers (`question_record_to_read`, `normalize_question_create`, etc.)
- Graph/artifact mappers (`graph_bundle_to_response`, `artifact_kind_for_key`, etc.)
- Worker job mappers (`worker_payload_to_job_state`, etc.)

These should be `question_mappers.py`, `graph_mappers.py`, `job_mappers.py`.

**`core/run_config.py` — 683 lines, 31 functions, mixed concerns**

Has two completely separate config domains — "run research" and "generate questions" — plus shared TOML loading helpers. Should be `run_research_config.py` and `generate_questions_config.py`.

**`packages/inference/llm/tiers.py` — 610 lines, 32 functions**

Three distinct concerns: type definitions (the enums/dataclasses), config file loading, and resolution functions (`resolve_task_model`, `resolve_tier_model` etc). The resolution functions alone are what everything imports — they're buried in a file that also handles raw TOML parsing.

**`packages/storage/sql_repositories.py` — 2,015 lines**

The function list already tells the story: question mappers up top, then graph/node/edge mappers, then `SqlGraphStoreRepository`. This is two repositories and their mappers in one file. `question_repository.py` and `graph_repository.py`.

---

**Genuine refactor opportunity — the three Fermi files**

`fermi_target.py`, `fermi_driver.py`, `fermi_indicator.py` are structurally identical: each has `_load_system_prompt()`, a `FermiXxxBaseline(BaseModel)` with the same fields, and `build_user_prompt()`. The only differences are the prompt name and minor field descriptions. A shared base class with abstract `_load_system_prompt()` would collapse ~200 lines of duplication across the three files.

---

**What's fine and doesn't need splitting**

`packages/contracts/questions.py` (338 lines, 24 classes) — lots of small types in one file is normal for a contracts module, that's its job. `core/research_driver/conditional_edge_quality.py` (645 lines, 3 classes) — the classes are tightly related, the size is okay. `packages/inference/llm/runtime.py` (1,260 lines) — complex but cohesive; it's all one runtime concern.

{"id":"SPI-252","title":"Code quality sweep: remove dead code, defensive wrapping, and env var sniffing across codebase","description":"## What this is\n\nSystematic sweep of the codebase for anti-patterns documented in `lookout.md`. The <issue id=\"15c6f655-f34e-4a27-8fff-d068f32f5913\">SPI-228</issue> port review surfaced several instances, but these patterns likely exist elsewhere too.\n\n## Anti-patterns to scan for\n\n1. **Dead code** — functions defined but never called, left behind with \"compatibility\" or \"legacy\" comments. Grep for callers; if none, delete.\n2. **Defensive** `str(x or \"\").strip()` **wrapping** — on values that are already typed strings. Adds noise, no safety benefit.\n3. `os.environ` **deep in domain code** — env vars read inside domain functions instead of at config/CLI edges.\n4. **Module-level model resolution constants** — `MY_MODEL = resolve_task_model(...)` at import time.\n5. **Inline system prompts** — hardcoded prompt strings instead of loading from `prompts/` via the prompt store.\n6. **Global mutation lazy imports** — `global _x` with `Any | None` sentinels instead of function-scoped imports.\n\n## Known instances (from <issue id=\"15c6f655-f34e-4a27-8fff-d068f32f5913\">SPI-228</issue> review)\n\n* `extract_causal_relationships()` in `indicator_extraction.py` — dead, never called (<issue id=\"f7cb8aa6-b26a-44c0-857c-aa627b2d4c73\">SPI-250</issue>)\n* `os.environ` in `_build_full_message_list()` in `indicator_extraction.py` (<issue id=\"f7cb8aa6-b26a-44c0-857c-aa627b2d4c73\">SPI-250</issue>)\n* Circular import lazy globals in `driver_indicator_literature.py` (<issue id=\"7ef805df-0d80-43d1-9edf-68379eddcfcc\">SPI-249</issue>)\n\n## Codebase scan findings (2026-05-12)\n\n### Defensive wrapping — 286 hits across non-test code\n\n`str(x or \"\")` pattern concentrated in storage mappers:\n\n* `packages/domain/qc/gating_store.py` — 14 instances\n* `packages/storage/sql_repositories.py` — 12 instances\n* `core/research_driver/data_model.py` — 12 instances\n* `packages/domain/qc/store.py` — 11 instances\n\n`dict(x or {})` wrapping: \\~45 hits across 20 files. Identity list comprehensions (`[item for item in list(...)]`): 4 files.\n\n### `os.environ` in domain code — 16 files\n\nOutside anything named config/settings/cli: `llm_scorer.py`, `relationship_judgment.py`, `fermi_llm.py`, `conditional_edge_quality.py`, `question_summary_pipeline.py`, and others.\n\n### Global mutation sentinels — 18 instances\n\nMostly `packages/inference/llm/runtime.py` (likely intentional lazy loading) but also `llm_scorer.py`, `fermi_llm.py`, `llm_prior.py`.\n\n### Module-level `resolve_task_model` — 2 remaining\n\n* `services/worker/agents/ResearchDriver/research_agent/web_search_client.py:37`\n* `core/research_driver/indicator_extraction.py:44`\n\n### Inline system prompts — 2 instances\n\n* `research/question_domains.py:132`\n* `core/pipelines/question_set_evaluation.py:257`\n\n### Zero-coverage files with real logic (from pytest --cov)\n\nOverall codebase coverage: 78%. Files at 0% that are not CLI entry points or external HTTP clients:\n\n| File | Stmts | Notes |\n| -- | -- | -- |\n| `core/pipelines/question_set_evaluation.py` | 497 | Large pipeline, may be superseded |\n| `packages/domain/qc/research_defensibility_store.py` | 117 | Domain storage |\n| `services/worker/agents/ResearchDriver/research_agent/web_search_client.py` | 138 | May be superseded |\n| `packages/domain/qc/track_record_loader.py` | 73 | Track record feature |\n| `packages/domain/qc/track_record_aware_judge.py` | 47 | Track record feature |\n| `fs10_validation/components/fs3_extraction.py` | 97 | Validation component |\n| `services/worker/run_config_context.py` | 17 | Nothing imports this |\n\nThe `track_record_loader` / `track_record_aware_judge` pair at 0% coverage with no callers looks like an incomplete or abandoned feature.\n\n### Backward-compat shims accumulating — 17 instances\n\nExplicit \"backward compat\" / \"will be removed\" comments in production code:\n\n* `packages/storage/database.py` lines 670, 2530, 2640 — two marked \"will be removed\"\n* `packages/storage/graph_store.py` lines 334, 380\n* `core/pipelines/graph_persistence.py:602`\n* `core/research_driver/indicator_extraction.py:587`\n* `core/research_driver/question_workflow.py:23`\n* `core/research_driver/driver_ontology.py:316`\n* `services/worker/research_driver_question_pipeline.py:641`\n* `packages/domain/baselines/apply.py:268`\n\n### AI-boilerplate module docstrings — 103 files\n\nFiles with the `Role: / Where It Is Used: / Relies On: / Key Symbols:` docstring format. This is a recognisable AI generation pattern that stands out immediately to any experienced reviewer. Should be replaced with a single concise sentence stating what the module does.\n\n### Monster functions\n\n| Function | File | Size |\n| -- | -- | -- |\n| `_run_single_step_impl()` | `services/worker/research_driver_step_pipeline.py:404` | 1,416 lines |\n| `run_research_driver_for_question()` | `services/worker/research_driver_question_pipeline.py:1861` | 1,205 lines |\n| `factor_extraction.run()` | `services/worker/agents/ResearchDriver/insight_agent/factor_extraction.py:731` | 533 lines |\n\n`run_research_driver_over_questions()` in `research_driver_batch_pipeline.py:396` takes **45 parameters**. This is a config object that was never extracted.\n\n### God files\n\n| File | Lines |\n| -- | -- |\n| `services/worker/agents/ResearchDriver/research_agent/retrieval_packs.py` | 3,380 |\n| `services/worker/research_driver_question_pipeline.py` | 3,066 |\n| `packages/storage/database.py` | 2,643 |\n| `packages/storage/sql_repositories.py` | 2,015 |\n| `services/worker/research_driver_step_pipeline.py` | 1,957 |\n\n`retrieval_packs.py` also has 33 local imports — it touches almost every layer of the stack.\n\n### Duplication\n\n* `build_messages()` defined **24 times** across the codebase — likely a protocol method that was copy-pasted instead of put on a base class\n* `postprocess()` defined **17 times**\n* `assess_candidate()`, `build_query()`, `search_candidates()`, `validate_scope_details()`, `iter_sources()` each appear 4–7 times inside `retrieval_packs.py` alone — strong signal of copy-pasted classes rather than a proper class hierarchy\n* `build_user_prompt()` in 4 separate Fermi files (`fermi_target.py`, `fermi_driver.py`, `fermi_indicator.py`, `fermi_edge.py`) — should be a base class method\n\n## Approach\n\nGrep the full codebase for each pattern, file tickets or fix in place depending on scope. Reference `lookout.md` as the authoritative list.\n\nHighest-priority items before any investor technical review:\n\n1. Strip the `Role: / Where It Is Used:` boilerplate from module docstrings (cosmetic but an immediate tell)\n2. Clean the storage mapper defensive wrapping (most visible per-file code smell)\n3. Break up `_run_single_step_impl()` and `run_research_driver_for_question()` — 1,000+ line functions are the clearest signal of unreviewed generated code\n4. Extract a config dataclass from the 45-parameter `run_research_driver_over_questions()` signature\n5. Refactor `retrieval_packs.py` copy-pasted classes into a proper hierarchy\n\n## Tooling\n\nTwo tools can accelerate the dead code portion of this sweep and should be run before manual review:\n\n`vulture` — static dead code detector for Python. Run with:\n\n```\nuvx vulture packages/ core/ services/ --min-confidence 80\n```\n\nNote: running against `.` hits a recursion error on the full tree; scope to source dirs only.\n\n`ruff --select F401` — catches unused imports:\n\n```\nuv run ruff check . --select F401\n```\n\n**Limitations of automated tools:**\n\n* Neither tool catches code that is reachable but never actually invoked in practice.\n* `vulture` will miss anything called via `getattr`, plugin registration, or string-based dispatch.\n* Manual grep for callers remains the authoritative check for any function flagged as suspicious.","priority":{"value":3,"name":"Medium"},"url":"https://linear.app/spire-elijah/issue/SPI-252/code-quality-sweep-remove-dead-code-defensive-wrapping-and-env-var","gitBranchName":"sumeet/spi-252-code-quality-sweep-remove-dead-code-defensive-wrapping-and","createdAt":"2026-05-12T14:55:23.345Z","updatedAt":"2026-05-12T17:46:54.430Z","archivedAt":null,"completedAt":null,"startedAt":null,"canceledAt":null,"dueDate":null,"slaStartedAt":null,"slaMediumRiskAt":null,"slaHighRiskAt":null,"slaBreachesAt":null,"slaType":"all","status":"Backlog","statusType":"backlog","labels":[],"attachments":[],"documents":[],"createdBy":"sumeet@sumeetsaini.com","createdById":"c7a8f74f-a7ba-41e8-b771-85fc97b42216","assignee":"sumeet@sumeetsaini.com","assigneeId":"c7a8f74f-a7ba-41e8-b771-85fc97b42216","project":"Elijah Pipeline Development","projectId":"cf9fb1a1-0344-45e5-933a-a689a00d3858","team":"Spire_Elijah","teamId":"6fc98d18-8a80-4c49-8c42-a6e2a4d3d722"}

Each has its own helper cluster. Should become `numeric_series.py`, `event_history.py`, `categorical_archive.py`.\n\n**`services/api/mappers.py` (755 lines, 27 functions)** — three distinct mapping concerns: question/question-set mappers, graph/artifact mappers, worker job mappers. Should become `question_mappers.py`, `graph_mappers.py`, `job_mappers.py`.\n\n**`core/run_config.py` (683 lines, 31 functions)** — two separate config domains (\"run research\" and \"generate questions\") plus shared TOML loading helpers. Should become `run_research_config.py` and `generate_questions_config.py`.\n\n**`packages/inference/llm/tiers.py` (610 lines, 32 functions)** — three concerns: type definitions (enums/dataclasses), config file loading, and resolution functions (`resolve_task_model` etc). The resolution functions are what everything imports; they're buried alongside raw TOML parsing.\n\n**`packages/storage/sql_repositories.py` (2,015 lines)** — question mappers/repository and graph/node/edge mappers/repository are two separate things. Should become `question_repository.py` and `graph_repository.py`.\n\n## Approach\n\nGrep the full codebase for each pattern, file tickets or fix in place depending on scope. Reference `lookout.md` as the authoritative list.\n\nHighest-priority items before any investor technical review:\n\n1. Strip the `Role: / Where It Is Used:` boilerplate from module docstrings (cosmetic but an immediate tell)\n2. Clean the storage mapper defensive wrapping (most visible per-file code smell)\n3. Break up `_run_single_step_impl()` and `run_research_driver_for_question()` — 1,000+ line functions are the clearest signal of unreviewed generated code\n4. Extract a config dataclass from the 45-parameter `run_research_driver_over_questions()` signature\n5. Refactor `retrieval_packs.py` copy-pasted classes into a proper hierarchy\n6. Extract a base class for the three `fermi_*.py` files\n\n## Tooling\n\nTwo tools can accelerate the dead code portion of this sweep and should be run before manual review:\n\n**`vulture`** — static dead code detector for Python. Run with:\n\n```\nuvx vulture packages/ core/ services/ --min-confidence 80\n```\n\nNote: running against `.` hits a recursion error on the full tree; scope to source dirs only.\n\n**`ruff --select F401`** — catches unused imports:\n\n```\nuv run ruff check . --select F401\n```\n\n**Limitations of automated tools:**\n\n- Neither tool catches code that is reachable but never actually invoked in practice.\n- `vulture` will miss anything called via `getattr`, plugin registration, or string-based dispatch.\n- Manual grep for callers remains the authoritative check for any function flagged as suspicious."
}

Should become `numeric_series.py`, `event_history.py`, `categorical_archive.py`.\n\n`services/api/mappers.py` **(755 lines, 27 functions)** — three distinct mapping concerns: question/question-set mappers, graph/artifact mappers, worker job mappers. Should become `question_mappers.py`, `graph_mappers.py`, `job_mappers.py`.\n\n`core/run_config.py` **(683 lines, 31 functions)** — two separate config domains (\"run research\" and \"generate questions\") plus shared TOML loading helpers. Should become `run_research_config.py` and `generate_questions_config.py`.\n\n`packages/inference/llm/tiers.py` **(610 lines, 32 functions)** — three concerns: type definitions (enums/dataclasses), config file loading, and resolution functions (`resolve_task_model` etc). The resolution functions are what everything imports; they're buried alongside raw TOML parsing.\n\n`packages/storage/sql_repositories.py` **(2,015 lines)** — question mappers/repository and graph/node/edge mappers/repository are two separate things. Should become `question_repository.py` and `graph_repository.py`.\n\n## Approach\n\nGrep the full codebase for each pattern, file tickets or fix in place depending on scope. Reference `lookout.md` as the authoritative list.\n\nHighest-priority items before any investor technical review:\n\n1. Strip the `Role: / Where It Is Used:` boilerplate from module docstrings (cosmetic but an immediate tell)\n2. Clean the storage mapper defensive wrapping (most visible per-file code smell)\n3. Break up `_run_single_step_impl()` and `run_research_driver_for_question()` — 1,000+ line functions are the clearest signal of unreviewed generated code\n4. Extract a config dataclass from the 45-parameter `run_research_driver_over_questions()` signature\n5. Refactor `retrieval_packs.py` copy-pasted classes into a proper hierarchy\n6. Extract a base class for the three `fermi_*.py` files\n\n## Tooling\n\nTwo tools can accelerate the dead code portion of this sweep and should be run before manual review:\n\n`vulture` — static dead code detector for Python. Run with:\n\n```\nuvx vulture packages/ core/ services/ --min-confidence 80\n```\n\nNote: running against `.` hits a recursion error on the full tree; scope to source dirs only.\n\n`ruff --select F401` — catches unused imports:\n\n```\nuv run ruff check . --select F401\n```\n\n**Limitations of automated tools:**\n\n* Neither tool catches code that is reachable but never actually invoked in practice.\n* `vulture` will miss anything called via `getattr`, plugin registration, or string-based dispatch.\n* Manual grep for callers remains the authoritative check for any function flagged as suspicious.","priority":{"value":3,"name":"Medium"},"url":"https://linear.app/spire-elijah/issue/SPI-252/code-quality-sweep-remove-dead-code-defensive-wrapping-and-env-var","gitBranchName":"sumeet/spi-252-code-quality-sweep-remove-dead-code-defensive-wrapping-and","createdAt":"2026-05-12T14:55:23.345Z","updatedAt":"2026-05-12T17:51:31.414Z","archivedAt":null,"completedAt":null,"startedAt":null,"canceledAt":null,"dueDate":null,"slaStartedAt":null,"slaMediumRiskAt":null,"slaHighRiskAt":null,"slaBreachesAt":null,"slaType":"all","status":"Backlog","statusType":"backlog","labels":[],"attachments":[],"documents":[],"createdBy":"sumeet@sumeetsaini.com","createdById":"c7a8f74f-a7ba-41e8-b771-85fc97b42216","assignee":"sumeet@sumeetsaini.com","assigneeId":"c7a8f74f-a7ba-41e8-b771-85fc97b42216","project":"Elijah Pipeline Development","projectId":"cf9fb1a1-0344-45e5-933a-a689a00d3858","team":"Spire_Elijah","teamId":"6fc98d18-8a80-4c49-8c42-a6e2a4d3d722"}

> DEVELOPER

so rifth now the team is going for a massive push to get features out so we can have a good demo ready and demonstrate that the product works and makes good predicitons. If someone is interested, how long do you think it'll take to get this "investor ready" and do you think our apporach is good becasue if no one is intrerested no point making the code super sexy

> AGENT

Honest answer: **your current approach is right.**

A working product that makes good predictions will get investor interest. Code quality gets you through due diligence after that interest exists — it almost never creates the interest in the first place. Especially at this stage, no one is going to pass on a product with compelling predictions because `_run_single_step_impl` is 1,400 lines.

---

**If someone IS interested — how long to get it investor-ready**

Depends what "interested" means:

- **Casual technical look** (a founder-friendly investor skimming GitHub): 2-3 days. The boilerplate docstrings are the main tell — that's a mechanical find-and-replace job, maybe a day. Clean the worst two or three mapper files. Done.

- **Technical advisor doing real due diligence** (Series A, someone who will read the pipeline code): 3-4 weeks of focused work. The monster functions, the `retrieval_packs.py` refactor, splitting the god files — these are meaningful engineering changes that need testing. You can't rush them safely.

- **CTO-level hire doing an evaluation**: They'll see through a cosmetic cleanup anyway. What will impress them is the architecture being sound (it is), the test coverage being real (78% is good), and no dead ends — which is more about being able to walk them through the system with confidence than the code looking pretty.

---

**The one thing I'd actually do now, regardless**

The 103 `Role: / Where It Is Used:` boilerplate docstrings. It takes half a day, it's zero risk, and it's the single most visible "AI wrote this and no one reviewed it" signal in the codebase. Everything else can wait until there's a reason to do it.

> DEVELOPER

3-4 weeks really? Even with AI?

> AGENT

Fair point. With AI doing the mechanical work, compress that significantly:

**Boilerplate docstrings — 1 day**
Pure find-and-replace with judgment. Spawn an agent per directory, done.

**Defensive wrapping (286 instances) — 2-3 days**
Mechanical but needs type-awareness to not break anything. Agent can do it file by file with tests running after each batch.

**Monster functions — this is the hard one**
`_run_single_step_impl` at 1,416 lines isn't slow to refactor because of typing speed — it's slow because you need to understand what it does before you cut it up. An AI that doesn't understand the domain will produce a refactor that looks clean but subtly breaks behaviour. This is the one place where AI speed doesn't fully apply. Call it a week with careful review at each step.

**File splits — 3-4 days**
Mechanical once the split points are clear (and we already identified them). Agent moves code, fixes imports, runs tests.

**`retrieval_packs.py` hierarchy — 3-4 days**
Needs design thought first, then mechanical. Similar constraint to the monster functions.

---

**Revised estimate: 2 weeks, not 4**

And honestly the stuff that matters visually — docstrings, defensive wrapping, file splits — is done in the first 3-4 days. The monster function refactor and `retrieval_packs.py` are the tail, and a technical reviewer looking at those files will at least see *intent* if the surrounding code is clean.

So if interest materialises: 3-4 days to look presentable, 2 weeks to be genuinely clean.

> AGENT

Here's the picture:

**`__init__.py` — the real problem is `packages/storage/__init__.py` at 455 lines**

That's a massive barrel file re-exporting everything from every storage submodule. The consequence is that callers write `from packages.storage import X` without knowing or caring where X actually lives — which makes the file split work harder (you have to update the barrel too), increases circular import risk, and means every import of the storage package pulls in a large chunk of the dependency graph.

The others (`domain/qc` at 131, `contracts` at 91, `inference/llm` at 87) are fine — those are legitimate public API surfaces for their packages. The `domain/qc` one actually has a good docstring explaining the design.

**Enums — not actually a problem**

Only one enum with 10+ values across the whole codebase: `ArtifactKind` at 13. That's fine. The enum situation is clean.

**`__all__` in non-`__init__` files — moderate concern**

`fs10_validation/calibration_sweep.py` has ~41 exports in `__all__`. A calibration sweep file shouldn't be exporting 41 things — that's a sign the file is doing too many jobs, which we already knew. `retrieval_packs.py` has ~24, again consistent with it being a god file. The `__all__` is a symptom, not the cause.

---

**Bottom line:** The `__init__.py` story is one real problem (`packages/storage/__init__.py`) that's directly related to the `sql_repositories.py` split we already identified — you'd tackle them together. The enums are fine. The `__all__` issue is noise from the god files, not a separate thing to fix.

Nothing here changes the estimate or the priority list. It's all downstream of the same root causes already in the ticket.

# Task 1: Elite Python — What Distinguishes It

## The Core Distinction

Elite Python is characterised by one discipline: **every line is intentional and the types tell the whole truth**. Average Python apologises for uncertainty with `Any`, defensive coercions, and silent fallbacks. Elite Python surfaces uncertainty at the boundary and refuses to propagate it inward.

---

## Principles (synthesised from research + production experience)

### 1. Types that carry meaning, not just labels

- `Any` is a lie to the type checker. Every `Any` in a function signature or dataclass field is a gap in the contract. Elite code uses `Any` only in genuine type-system bootstrap situations (ORM internals, `**kwargs` passthrough with explicit narrowing), never as a convenience.
- `Optional[X]` spam (`X | None` everywhere) is the cousin of `Any`: it defers the question of emptiness to every caller. The question should be answered at the boundary, not smeared across the interior.
- `NewType` encodes semantic identity: `RunId = NewType("RunId", str)` means the type checker rejects a raw `str` where a `RunId` is required. This costs nothing at runtime and catches entire classes of transposition bugs.
- `Literal["pending", "graded", "llm_error"]` is better than `str` for a status field. The string enum `StrEnum` is better still because it is a checked enumeration, not a free-form label.
- `TypeAlias` + `type` statements (Python 3.12+) for complex shapes: `StepMap: TypeAlias = dict[str, dict[str, Any]]` at least localises the `Any` and names the concept.

### 2. `dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)` — know when and why

- `frozen=True` makes instances hashable and prevents mutation after construction, which is the right default for value objects. If something should be mutable, that should be a deliberate choice.
- `slots=True` (Python 3.10+) eliminates `__dict__`, reduces memory per instance, speeds attribute access. Zero reason not to use it for value types.
- The performance cost of `frozen=True` (2.4x slower instantiation) matters for hot paths. For DTO/value-object construction that happens once per request, irrelevant. Elite engineers know which path they're on.
- The anti-pattern: using a `frozen=True` dataclass and then putting mutable containers (`list`, `dict`) as fields — the field reference is immutable, the contents are not. Truly immutable value objects use `tuple` and `frozenset`.

### 3. Protocol over ABC

- `ABC` creates nominal subtype coupling: a class must explicitly inherit from the ABC to satisfy it. This forces every implementation to know about the abstract class.
- `Protocol` uses structural subtyping (duck typing with type-checker enforcement). A class satisfies a protocol by having the right methods, regardless of inheritance hierarchy. This is the correct model for Python.
- Use `ABC` only when you need guaranteed `abstractmethod` enforcement at construction time and you control the entire hierarchy. Use `Protocol` when you want to describe an interface that third-party or generated types might satisfy without modification.
- `runtime_checkable` on a Protocol adds `isinstance` support at a cost: only method presence is checked, not signatures.

### 4. Functional core, imperative shell

- Pure functions take values, return values, have no side effects, and are trivially testable. They form the **core**.
- I/O, DB writes, context managers, time, random: these belong in the **shell**, which orchestrates pure functions.
- The failure mode is mixing: a "pure" computation function that also writes to the DB, or a `load_` function that also calls `create_all()` as a side effect.
- In practice: every function that returns a domain object (scorecard, baseline, QC step) should be pure and take all its inputs as arguments. Functions that touch the DB, filesystem, or network are shell functions and should be clearly named and segregated.

### 5. Error handling — no bare `except`, no swallowed exceptions

- `except Exception` with no re-raise is a bug factory. It converts errors into silent state.
- `except Exception as exc:` with `return {"status": "error", "message": str(exc)}` is marginally better but still swallows the traceback and type information.
- Elite pattern: let the exception propagate, catch it at the outermost layer (API handler, CLI entrypoint), log with full context, and surface a typed error response. The interior is exception-clean.
- `assert` in production path code is wrong: it is disabled with `-O` and is not a substitute for validation.

### 6. No defensive coercions in the interior

The pattern `str(x or "").strip()` repeated 40 times in a function is a red flag. It says: "I don't trust my inputs and I'm not going to enforce the contract." Elite code instead:
- Validates and normalises at the boundary (API layer, deserialisation).
- Trusts the interior: if `run_id` is already a `str` by the type system, no interior coercion needed.
- If `None` is genuinely possible, the type says `str | None` and each access is explicit.

### 7. No hidden state

- Module-level mutable globals, `ContextVar` mutations that are not in an explicit `with` block, functions that modify shared dictionaries — all of these are hidden state.
- Hidden state makes functions non-pure and destroys testability. Test isolation requires either injecting state or patching it, both of which are symptoms.

### 8. Earned abstractions

An abstraction is earned when it removes more complexity than it introduces. The Jane Street discipline (applied to OCaml, but transferable): every abstraction should pay for itself in reduced surface area or improved correctness. A function that is called once is not an abstraction, it is fragmentation. A class that wraps a single dict is not encapsulation, it is ceremony.

### 9. Names that are true

- `_ensure_database` does two things: it resolves and it creates. `resolve_and_init_database` is honest.
- `load_` functions that also write side effects violate the principle of least surprise.
- `normalized_run_id` as a local variable in every function means the normalisation should be a type boundary, not a repeated local ritual.

---

# Task 2: File-Level Findings

---

## `/Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah/packages/domain/qc/store.py`

### Dataclass fields: `Any` everywhere, types lie

**Lines 29–33** (`StoredQCStep`):
```
input_json: dict[str, Any] | None
output_json: dict[str, Any] | None
artifacts: list[dict[str, Any]]
llm_trace_stats: dict[str, Any] | None
```
`dict[str, Any]` is a type-system lie. These fields have known shapes — QC step inputs and outputs are domain structures. At minimum, define `type StepPayload = dict[str, object]` or, better, use typed dataclasses/Pydantic models for the contents. `Any` propagates: every caller of these fields is forced to use `Any` too, and the type checker provides zero guarantees downstream.

**Lines 25–26** (`status: str`, `started_at: str`, `ended_at: str`):
`status` should be a `Literal["pending", "running", "done", "failed"]` or a `StrEnum`. A raw `str` means any string is valid. `started_at` and `ended_at` as `str` is a temporal precision lie — these are timestamps; `datetime | None` with UTC discipline is the correct type, with serialisation to string at the boundary (API layer), not at the storage layer.

**Line 45** (`index_json: dict[str, Any]`): Same problem. This is a structured document, not a free-form dict.

**Line 62** (`axes: dict[str, Any]`): The axes of a QC scorecard are not `Any`. They have a known schema. Even `dict[str, float]` would be better if the axes values are numeric scores.

### `_ensure_database`: misleading name, hidden side effect (line 67)

```python
def _ensure_database(config: StorageRuntimeConfig | None = None):
```

This function:
1. Resolves the database (pure-ish)
2. Calls `create_all()` — a **write side effect**

The name says "ensure" but the action is "create all tables if they don't exist." This violates the functional core principle: any function named `_ensure_X` that does I/O without being explicitly in the shell layer is leaking. The return type is also unannotated — no `-> Database` (or whatever the actual type is).

More critically: this is called at the top of every read function (`load_question_qc`, `list_question_scorecards`). A read operation should not trigger schema creation as a side effect. Schema initialisation belongs at application startup, not inside every storage read.

### Defensive coercions: ~40 instances of `str(x or "").strip()` (lines 85–86, 103–115, 163–164, 219–220, etc.)

This pattern:
```python
normalized_run_id = str(run_id or "").strip()
normalized_question_id = str(question_id or "").strip()
```

...repeated in every function body is a symptom that the type boundary is wrong. The signatures say `run_id: str` and `question_id: str` — so why are they being coerced from potentially `None` or non-`str` inputs? Either:
1. The callers pass garbage and the type annotations are lying, or
2. The coercions are unnecessary defensive code.

If (1), fix the callers and enforce at the API boundary. If (2), delete the coercions. Repeating this defensive ritual in 5 functions is not defence-in-depth, it is type-system distrust expressed as code volume.

**Line 104**:
```python
export_index_path=str(export_index_path or "").strip() or None,
```
`str(...) or None` after `str(... or "")` — this is a triple-escape hatch. If the field is `str | None`, then `None` is valid and should be passed directly. The coercion chain obscures what the actual value can be.

**Line 105**:
```python
index_json=dict(index_json or {}),
```
`index_json` is typed as `dict[str, Any]`. `dict(index_json or {})` makes a shallow copy of what is already a dict. If the intent is defensive copying to prevent mutation of the caller's dict, say so explicitly. If it's paranoia about `None`, that's a type lie — the parameter is annotated `dict[str, Any]`, not `dict[str, Any] | None`.

### `assert stored is not None` (line 152)

This is using `assert` as a runtime error guard in production code. `assert` is disabled with `-O`. If the read after write is genuinely expected to succeed, a proper `raise RuntimeError(...)` is correct. If it can fail, handle the failure explicitly.

### Deferred import inside function body (line 308)

```python
from .gating_store import persist_graph_concerns_and_gate
```

This import is deferred to avoid a circular import. That is a signal that the module graph has a cycle that should be broken by extracting the shared dependency, not by deferring the import. Deferred imports are a code smell indicating architectural coupling problems.

### `persist_graph_scorecards`: `graph: Any` (line 215)

The function takes `graph: Any` and extracts data from it via `getattr` with defaults scattered across lines 220, 232–233, 247. This is a fully untyped interface to a domain object. The graph has a type somewhere in the codebase. Pass the typed object, or define a `Protocol` with the fields this function needs. `getattr(graph, "run_id", "")` in production domain logic is using Python's dynamic dispatch as a type workaround.

### `list(getattr(graph, "nodes", {}).values())` (line 232)

`getattr` with a default dict means: "I don't know if this object has a `nodes` attribute and I'm not going to find out." If `graph` had a proper type (even a `Protocol`), this would be `graph.nodes.values()` and the type checker would know the shape.

---

## `/Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah/packages/domain/baselines/fermi_target.py` and `fermi_driver.py`

These two files are near-identical. That's the first problem.

### Module-level side effect at import time (line 94 / line 96)

```python
# fermi_target.py line 94
__all__ = ["FermiTargetBaseline", "build_user_prompt"]
SYSTEM_PROMPT = _load_system_prompt()
```

`SYSTEM_PROMPT = _load_system_prompt()` executes at import time, unconditionally. `_load_system_prompt()` calls `default_prompt_store().load(...)` which hits the filesystem. This means:
- Importing this module has a side effect (filesystem read)
- Tests that import this module will hit the filesystem unless the store is mocked
- Any import-time failure (missing prompt file) crashes the entire Python process at import

`SYSTEM_PROMPT` is also placed **after** `__all__`, which is backwards — `__all__` should be last, or `SYSTEM_PROMPT` should be in `__all__` if it's part of the public interface. It's not in `__all__`, which means it's a module-level private-but-exported name.

The correct pattern: load lazily in `build_user_prompt` with `@lru_cache`, or pass the system prompt as a parameter from the shell layer.

### Duplication: `fermi_target.py` and `fermi_driver.py` are structurally identical

Both files define:
- A `FermiXBaseline(BaseModel)` with the same fields (only `description` differs)
- An identical `build_user_prompt(...)` function signature
- An identical `_load_system_prompt()` function
- The same module-level `SYSTEM_PROMPT` side effect

The only behavioural differences are the prompt path and the word "target" vs "driver" in the strings. This is a DRY violation that will require duplicate fixes every time the shared structure changes. A single `FermiNodeBaseline` base class or a `build_fermi_prompt(node_type: Literal["target", "driver"], ...)` factory would eliminate the duplication.

### `Mapping[str, Any]` (line 62 / line 69 in both files)

`ontology: Mapping[str, Any]` — `Any` again. The ontology has known keys (`support_level`, `evidence_grade`, etc., enumerated in the loop body on lines 72–76). Define a `TypedDict` or `Protocol` for the ontology shape:
```python
class NodeOntology(TypedDict, total=False):
    support_level: str
    evidence_grade: str
    observability: str
    clock_source: str
    surprise: str
    temporal_form: str
    scope: str
```
This turns the loop's `.get(key, "")` into type-checked field access and documents the schema inline.

### `description or 'No description provided.'` (line 85 / fermi_target.py line 85)

If `description` can be empty and needs a fallback, the parameter should be `description: str = ""` and the fallback should be in the function body once, not inline in the f-string. The current pattern mixes data normalisation with string formatting.

### Horizon text (line 69 / line 71)

```python
horizon_text = f"{horizon_days} days" if horizon_days else (horizon_bucket or "unknown")
```

`horizon_days: int | None` — if it's `0` (which is a valid `int`), this branch goes to `horizon_bucket`, not `"0 days"`. The check should be `if horizon_days is not None` to handle zero correctly. This is a classic `if x` vs `if x is not None` bug.

---

## `/Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah/packages/inference/llm/tiers.py` (first 200 lines)

### Redundant aliases (lines 25–27, 82–83)

```python
TIER_SMALL = LLMTier.SMALL
TIER_MEDIUM = LLMTier.MEDIUM
TIER_LARGE = LLMTier.LARGE
```
These are module-level aliases for enum members. If the enum is imported directly, these are unnecessary. If they exist for backward compat, document that. Currently they look like noise.

```python
LLMProfileSelection = LLMModelSetSelection
LLMProfileCapabilities = LLMModelSetCapabilities
```
Type aliases for backward compat, but they're naked assignments with no `TypeAlias` annotation (Python 3.10+) or `type` statement (3.12+). The type checker cannot distinguish a type alias from a value assignment here.

### `LLMModelSetCapabilities.as_dict()` (lines 59–66)

```python
def as_dict(self) -> dict[str, Any]:
    return {
        "structured_output": self.structured_output,
        ...
    }
```

`dict[str, Any]` return type when all the values have known types (`bool | None`, `int | None`, `str | None`). This should be a `TypedDict` or the method should be replaced with `dataclasses.asdict()`. Additionally, `as_dict()` on a frozen dataclass is redundant — `dataclasses.asdict(instance)` gives you this without a custom method.

### `LLMTaskDefinition.as_dict()` (lines 75–79)

```python
def as_dict(self) -> dict[str, str]:
    return {
        "tier": self.tier.value,
        "description": self.description,
    }
```
Returns `dict[str, str]` but omits `task_id`. If this is for serialisation, it loses information. If it's for a specific use, the use should be explicit. Also: `dataclasses.asdict` would include all fields; the manual dict is either intentionally partial (document why) or accidentally lossy.

### `model_set_context` / `llm_profile_context` (lines 86–99)

`llm_profile_context` is a pure wrapper around `model_set_context` with a different name. It exists for a deprecated API surface. This is fine, but it should be annotated `@deprecated` or the docstring should say "deprecated alias for `model_set_context`". Currently it looks like an independent concept.

### Defensive coercions in `normalize_tier` (lines 111–118)

```python
def normalize_tier(value: str | LLMTier) -> LLMTier:
    if isinstance(value, LLMTier):
        return value
    text = str(value or "").strip().lower()
```

`value: str | LLMTier` — the type says `value` is either a `str` or an `LLMTier`. If it's a `str`, `str(value or "")` is still defensive coercion: `value` is already a `str` by this branch. The `or ""` guards against... an empty string, which is a valid `str`. The correct pattern is just `text = value.strip().lower()` since `isinstance(value, LLMTier)` has already been handled.

### `resolve_model_set_selection`: triple-env-var fallback chain (lines 121–146)

The function has 6 resolution sources in priority order. This is justified complexity for environment-driven configuration. The issue is the defensive coercions at every step:
```python
explicit_model_set = str(model_set or "").strip()
deprecated_profile = str(llm_profile or "").strip()
active = str(get_active_model_set_name() or "").strip()
env_model_set = str(os.environ.get(LLM_MODEL_SET_ENV, "") or "").strip()
```
`model_set: str | None` — if it's `None`, `str(None or "")` = `""` then `.strip()` = `""`. Fine. But `str(model_set or "")` is redundant when you could write `model_set or ""` since it's already typed as `str | None`. The `str()` call is noise that obscures the intent. Better:
```python
if model_set:
    return LLMModelSetSelection(name=model_set.strip(), source="explicit")
```

### `_load_llm_profile_capabilities_cached` return type (line 244)

```python
) -> tuple[tuple[str, dict[str, Any]], ...]:
```
The `dict[str, Any]` is the serialised capabilities map. This is a cache-internal detail, but `Any` leaks out and forces the caller to re-parse. The return type should use a proper `CapabilityMap: TypeAlias = dict[str, bool | int | str | None]` to at least narrow the value type.

---

## `/Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah/core/research_driver/conditional_edge_quality.py` (first ~250 lines)

### `ConditionalEdgeQualityTool.build_messages`: inline prompt (lines 68–84)

```python
def build_messages(self, *, edge_context: Mapping[str, Any], **_: Any) -> list[dict[str, str]]:
    prompt = (
        "Grade this CONDITIONAL edge on two dimensions, each from 1 to 5.\n\n"
        ...
    )
```

This is a system prompt baked into the source code as a multiline string. The architectural pattern this codebase uses is `default_prompt_store().load(...)` — see line 115 (`FermiConditionalEdgeQualityTool`) which correctly loads from the prompt store. The `ConditionalEdgeQualityTool` is inconsistent: it hardcodes its prompt. This is exactly the "inline system prompt" anti-pattern called out in the porting anti-patterns guide.

### `postprocess` signature: `result: Any` (lines 86, 129)

Both tools' `postprocess` methods take `result: Any`. The `response_model` class attribute tells you exactly what type `result` should be: `_ConditionalEdgeQualityOutput` for the first tool, `FermiOddsMultiplier` for the second. The `Any` forces the duck-typing checks (`hasattr(result, "model_dump")`, `isinstance(result, dict)`) that should be unnecessary. If the base class `BaseLLMTool` uses `Any` here, that is the root type-system gap.

### `postprocess` duck-typing on `result` (lines 87–91)

```python
if hasattr(result, "model_dump"):
    payload = result.model_dump(mode="json")
elif isinstance(result, dict):
    payload = dict(result)
else:
    payload = {}
```

This is defensive code that handles three possible shapes of `result`: a Pydantic model, a dict, or anything else (silent empty dict). The third branch is the dangerous one — if `result` is none of the above, it silently returns an empty dict and the caller sees `None` for all fields. This should raise, not silently swallow.

### `FermiConditionalEdgeQualityTool.postprocess`: bare `except Exception` (lines 136–143)

```python
try:
    combined = combine_odds(result)
except Exception as exc:  # pragma: no cover — defensive guard
    return {
        "status": EDGE_QUALITY_STATUS_LLM_ERROR,
        "error": f"combine_odds failed: {type(exc).__name__}: {exc}",
        ...
    }
```

This swallows the exception and converts it to a dict entry. The comment `# pragma: no cover — defensive guard` acknowledges this is never tested. An untested except branch that swallows exceptions is a silent failure path. If `combine_odds` can fail, its failure modes should be typed and handled explicitly, not caught by `Exception`.

### `_compact_value`: `result: Any` pervasion (lines 207–237)

```python
def _compact_value(value: Any, *, depth: int = 0) -> Any:
```

Both input and output are `Any`. The function handles `Mapping`, `list | tuple`, `str`, `int | float | bool | None`, and a catch-all. The return type is actually `str | int | float | bool | None | dict[str, ...] | list[...]`. This is a legitimate use of `Any` in a generic compaction utility, but the return type could be expressed as `str | int | float | bool | dict[str, object] | list[object] | None` which is more honest and enables downstream narrowing.

### `_node_context`: `graph: Any` (line 240)

Same graph-as-`Any` problem as in `store.py`. The graph object is a domain entity that appears throughout the codebase but is typed as `Any` wherever it's passed. This is the codebase's single largest type-system gap: the central graph object has no Protocol.

### Import of private symbols from `graph_store` (line 27)

```python
from packages.storage.graph_store import EdgeRecord, NodeRecord, _edges_payload, _write_json, load_edges, load_nodes
```

`_edges_payload` and `_write_json` are module-private (underscore prefix). Importing private symbols from another module couples this file to the internal implementation of `graph_store`. If these are needed, they should be made public (and added to `__all__`) or moved to a shared utility module.

---

## `/Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah/packages/storage/database.py` (lines 1–100)

### `_storage_alembic_config` return type: `Any` (line 71)

```python
def _storage_alembic_config(*, database_url: str | None = None) -> Any:
```

Returns `Any` when the return type is `alembic.config.Config`. The `from alembic.config import Config` import is deferred (inside the function body, line 73), which is why `Any` is used as the return type. The deferred import could be moved to the module level, or a `TYPE_CHECKING` guard used to enable type-checking without import overhead.

### `_alembic_head_revisions`: defensive coercions (line 85)

```python
heads = tuple(sorted(str(revision).strip() for revision in script.get_heads() if str(revision).strip()))
```

`str(revision).strip()` is called twice on the same value: once in the filter, once in the transform. This is inefficient and hard to read. The correct pattern:
```python
heads = tuple(sorted(r for rev in script.get_heads() if (r := str(rev).strip())))
```
Or more readably, extract to a comprehension with a named intermediate. The double-`str()` call suggests uncertainty about the type returned by `script.get_heads()` — if it's `Iterator[str]`, no coercion needed.

### `utc_now()` (line 67)

Good: `datetime.now(UTC)` is correct and explicit. No issues.

### `StorageSchemaNotReady(RuntimeError)` (line 63)

Good: a typed exception class rather than bare `RuntimeError`. This is the right pattern.

---

## `/Users/sumeet/matrix/elijah/packages/contracts/questions.py`

This file is overall the cleanest of the set. Pydantic models, `extra="forbid"`, `StrEnum`, good field defaults. The issues are narrower:

### `buckets: list[Any] | None = None` (line 44, `QuestionRead`)

`list[Any]` is imprecise. Buckets are a domain concept with a shape — likely numeric intervals or labelled ranges. Even `list[float | str]` would be more honest.

### `data: dict[str, Any]` (line 45, `QuestionRead`)

Free-form data blob on a read DTO. This is a migration artifact — the `data` field is being used as a catch-all. In a well-typed system, every field in `data` that is actually used by the frontend or downstream consumers should be a typed field on the DTO. A `dict[str, Any]` on a DTO is an escape hatch that defeats the purpose of typed contracts.

### `target_type: str` on `QuestionDraftResponse` (line 162)

Comment says `# "binary" | "categorical" | "numeric_ordinal" | "time_ordinal"` — this should be a `Literal` or `StrEnum`, not a `str` with a comment.

### `classification: str = "binary"` (multiple places: lines 215, 263, 265, 278, 293)

`classification` is used as a free-form `str` with default `"binary"`. This appears in `DriverPromotionExecuteRequest`, `ConfirmedSubQuestion`, `NLQuestionSetProposal`, etc. If there are known valid values, they should be a `Literal` or `StrEnum`. Using `str` with an implicit "valid values are..." convention means all validation is deferred to runtime/DB constraints.

### `relevance: str = "direct"` (lines 256, 280)

Same issue: `"direct"` is one of presumably 2–3 values. Should be `Literal["direct", "indirect", ...]` or a `StrEnum`.

### `QuestionUpdateRequest` is a near-duplicate of `QuestionCreateRequest` (lines 100–144)

All fields in `QuestionUpdateRequest` are `X | None = None` versions of the fields in `QuestionCreateRequest`. This is the PATCH-vs-POST pattern, which is legitimate. However, maintaining two parallel lists of ~15 fields is fragile — adding a field to `Create` requires remembering to add it to `Update`. A common pattern is to generate `Update` from `Create` using Pydantic's `model_fields` introspection or a shared base, or to use `Optional`-wrapping utilities.

### `model_used: str = ""` (lines 202, 273)

`model_used` is a string field with a default of `""`. An empty string as a sentinel for "not set" is worse than `str | None = None` because the empty string is a valid string and can't be distinguished from "we have a value and it's empty." Use `None` to mean "not set."

---

## Cross-Cutting Pattern: The `graph: Any` Gap

This is the single most consequential type-system gap in the files reviewed. The graph domain object is passed as `Any` into:
- `store.py:persist_graph_scorecards(graph: Any, ...)`
- `conditional_edge_quality.py:_node_context(graph: Any, ...)`
- And presumably many more functions

Every `getattr(graph, "nodes", {})`, `getattr(graph, "run_id", "")`, etc. is untyped dynamic dispatch. The fix is a `Protocol`:

```python
class GraphLike(Protocol):
    run_id: str
    updated_at: str
    nodes: Mapping[str, NodeLike]
```

This would eliminate dozens of `getattr` calls and enable the type checker to catch field name typos and shape mismatches.

---
